#6892. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

#6889. You Can Definitely Do These Easy Nail Art Designs at Home

Take it from a fellow amateur manicurist.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Ah, the at-home manicure. Nail artists make it look so easy when you’re in the salon, but get in front of a file and dotting tool at home? Suddenly, things can get a little shaky. I’ve technically been doing my own nails since I was about six, but really started leaning into trying to get better at painting on little designs over the past few years. I can say from experience: There is a lot of so-called easy nail art out there that, in reality, is very difficult to do yourself. But there are also plenty of designs you can totally pull off as a beginner. That, my friends, is what we’re here for today.

Unfortunately, the old “practice makes perfect” thing does actually apply to any type of creative endeavor, manicures included. I’m not saying you’re going to be able to perfectly recreate any of these looks the first time you try (though, if you can, maybe quit your day job). There’s a reason I still pay a professional to do my nails if I want them to look extra super good for a special occasion; those people have years of training I’ll never be able to catch up to. Instead, these are designs with elements I know a beginner can do well enough that you won’t get so frustrated with the results that you give up on your new hobby for good.

“It can be super intimidating to start painting your nails while you're looking at an inspo pic or video from IG or Pinterest, but remember that the reason you probably started painting your nails was to have a creative outlet and a moment of self expression,” nail artist and wise person Sigourney Nuñez says. “Try to not let perfectionism get in the way of something that is meant to be a self care moment where you get to show up for yourself in this fun, creative and colorful way.”

Hear, hear! If you’re ready to get started on your artistic ungual journey, keep scrolling for expert-recommended tips, tools, and plenty of design inspiration.

While your toolkit can grow exponentially the further you get into your nail art journey, Nuñez says the “perfect DIY tool trifecta” is a clean up brush, dotting tool, and liner brush.

I can confirm the clean up brush will be your most-used tool when you’re learning to do your own nails. Even if you don’t make a massive, colored-outside-the-lines mess, going around your nail with a clean up brush dipped in nail polish remover “can really give you that crispy salon finish at home,” Nuñez says. She uses a Simply Simmons #4 painter’s brush you can buy at Michael’s, but says you could also sacrifice an old angled eyeliner brush if you aren’t looking to spend money on tools yet. “Just be sure you don’t use it on your eyes again after you’ve dunked it in nail polish remover,” she warns.

Nuñez calls dotting tools “the gateway to the nail art universe.” You could use bobby pins, a flat eraser on the end of a pencil, or the end of a brush, but there are plenty of affordable dotting tool options too. Olive & June makes a nice double-sided one that would be a great first dotting tool, while Halo Taco has a fun, holographic set that comes with a total of eight different sized tips.

Once you’re really ready to level up, get yourself a nail brush. There are sets with multiple brush sizes out there, but Nuñez doesn’t think you need them. “I literally only use one type of liner brush, [the] Liner 3 from Bio Seaweed Gel,” she says. “It’s been my go-to brush for years.” Instead of dealing with a bunch of different size brushes, you can make different size lines by varying the amount of pressure you use.

I cannot emphasize this enough: If you’re serious about wanting your manicure to look as close to professionally done as possible, you absolutely must prep your nails before you even think about picking up your base coat. This means completely removing all your polish, cutting and/or shaping your nails with a file, and cleaning up your cuticle area... but not too much on that last part.

“When it comes to DIY prep, less is more,” Nuñez says. “There is no need to over trim any surrounding skin unless there's a hangnail that's waving hi at you.” Instead, she recommends using a cuticle buffer stick like the Sally Hansen Push & Buff 2-in-1 Cuticle Pen. “It helps exfoliate the cuticle area and side walls, plus helps buff out the waxy layer that can accumulate [on your nails],” Nuñez says. “That waxy layer is totally normal but it gets in the way of adhesion.” I like to wash my hands after I’ve done all that trimming, filing, and buffing, to make sure there’s no leftover nail dust that could ruin my hard work.

Keeping your cuticles healthy between manis will make this prep step easier. “I’m actually so passionate about cuticle care,” Brooklyn-based nail artist Anna Miles says. “A little attention every day will do wonders—more than any nail tech could accomplish in our monthly sessions.” She recommends using a towel to gentle scrub your cuticle area after a shower (”to encourage the cuticle skin to roll back”) and be diligent about using hand cream and cuticle oil after you wash your hands.

“If you’re like me and out of sight equals out of mind, buy travel sizes in multiples and keep one everywhere—bag, coffee table, bathroom, bedside,” Miles says. She loves the CND Rescue Rxx Daily Keratin Treatment and Zoya Healing Dry Skin Hand & Body Cream, both of which come in smaller sizes.

The number one thing that will make your nail art look better is to learn to leverage your fulcrum finger (aka the digit that’s holding your hand steady while you work; Nuñez says this is usually the pinky) and elbows. Do not try to do your nails while sitting on a couch; you need a table with enough space to create those stabilizing points.

Whether you’re going to use a dotting tool, liner brush, sponge, or other nail art tool, it can take some practice to figure out how much polish to put on it before you start designing. I always do a test run on a piece of paper; it’s not a perfect substitution for painting on your curved nail, but I think it gives you a pretty good idea of how much polish to use. Nuñez also has a handy video on how to load your liner brush properly.

And, again, you just need to practice. When I’m in the habit of doing my nails every week, I’m shocked at how much better I get over the course of a month. Ready to dive in? Here’s some inspo to get you started.

Let’s start with the easiest nail art of them all, a skittles manicure. This is simply painting each nail with a different shade of polish, which is a fun way to start playing around with color theory—a key skill for professional-looking DIY manis. “Every great manicure starts with a beautiful color story,” Nuñez says. “When the colors can do most of the talking, you don't have to try as hard with your DIY design.”

Glitter polish is kind of a nail art hack in its own right, because the texture makes any mistakes kinda blend in. For this look, I swiped some random splotches of purple cream over a glitter from Mooncat (a brand that makes some of the best sparkles, IMO).

The half and half manicure is a great way to practice creating straight lines: Since you fill in the space on one side of the line, it’s less obvious if things are little crooked.

“When painting lines, instead of moving the brush, you can try rotating your hand/nail while keeping the brush anchored,” Nuñez advises. “This can help with a more precise application.”

I know, I know, all these straight lines look intimidating, but, similar to the half and half, I promise that the fact that you fill in the triangles after you paint on the lines makes it far easier to get a crisp look. And if you have the patience, you could always wait for the base color to try, then use Scotch tape to stencil out the details.

Marble looks complicated, but Miles swears it’s not so difficult. “Marble is good for beginners because the success of this design is in the imperfection,” she is. “Uneven, shaky lines and blurry, smeared areas... sounds like the makings of a gorgeous marble!” Pick two colors of polish with high contrast to make the marble design stand out.

Miles says there are many ways to do marble, but one of the first methods she fell in love with is using an unused kitchen sponge that’s been cut into thin slices. Start by applying one of your polishes as a base coat, then paint the other color onto a nail-sized section of your sponge slice. “You can see the marble ‘veins’ on the sponge as you paint it,” Miles says. Stamp the wet paint onto your nail, using a rocking motion so it covers the whole nail. You can add metallic accents or a shimmery top coat to really make it special.

This is one of my go-to designs. You paint on two coats on your base color, then go in with a second color but stop before you get too close to your cuticle. It’s extremely simple, but gets loads of compliments.

If you crave more intricate designs but don’t have the brush skillset just yet, stickers are a great option. “I love using nail art stickers,” Miles says, noting that her favorites come from Deco Beauty. “For best results, make sure to apply them to a long-dried base color and paint two thin top coats instead of one thick one if you’re using traditional polish.”

Polka dot nails stay trendy, so that dotting tool is definitely worth the investment. There are many different ways to do dots—a bunch of super tiny dots, big circles, a mix and match of multiple size. I’d recommend practicing on a piece of paper first so you can get the pressure just right for the size you’re looking for. A light touch is key once you’re actually on the nail. “If your base color is not fully dry and you put too much force on the dotting tool that can create a dent and hole in your hard work,” Nuñez says.

This is a design that definitely requires Nuñez’s recommended stabilization techniques, but if things get a little shaky, you can just say you were going for a Bambi effect. Larger dots can also be a bit more forgiving.

Another trick for adding details when you don’t have a super steady hand is to apply the color you want for the detail as the base coat and then layer thick strokes of another color on top. That’s what I did for this look: The blue polish went down first, then I just painted one strip of green vertically and one strip horizontally to create the rectangle accent effect.

As everything gets digitized, I fear we’ll lose important ancient techniques, like using paper hole protectors as a stencil for a negative space half moon effect. At least, that’s how I’d pull off this look.

In the vein of nail stickers, confetti polish is a great way to create nail art without even looking at an extra tool. (KB Shimmer makes really fun ones that come in a bunch of shapes and colors.) Just make sure to apply two thin coats of top coat so the sparkles stay put.

I would never in a million years suggest that a French manicure is easy. But I will tell you that those rounded silicone stamper tools absolutely work to create a micro French, especially on shorter nails.

Using a rhinestone application tool can be a bit tricky to master, but I do find it a relatively easy way to add just a touch of detail to your nails. These won’t stick with just some good top coat; you’ll need to use nail glue too.

Great news: Once you’ve gotten good at polka dots, you can totally do flowers too. These are made up of just five black dots and one white one, but the final look seems much more complicated.

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo with over 15 years of experience writing professionally about nails. She’s been an amateur nail artist for over 30.

#6887. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

#6883. The 2027 Met Gala Has a New Theme After the John Galliano Show Was Canceled

Next year’s exhibition will explore how designers turn wardrobe staples and ordinary materials into something worthy of a museum.

The 2027 Met Gala has a new theme. On November 5, the Metropolitan Museum of Art announced “extraoRDINARY: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday,” a Costume Institute exhibition exploring how designers transform everyday clothes and materials into something unexpected. The announcement comes more than two months after the museum canceled its planned John Galliano retrospective. Here’s everything we know so far.

The starting point for next year’s exhibition is the everyday wardrobe. “extraORDINARY: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday” will examine how designers rethink familiar pieces, including jeans, T-shirts, hoodies, trench coats, and puffers, as well as how they turn materials such as paper, plastic, and metal into clothing. The exhibition will also consider how wearing, styling, and caring for clothes gives them meaning over time. Recycling, upcycling, and reuse will be recurring subjects throughout.

The exhibition will open to the public at the Metropolitan Museum of Art on May 9, 2027, and run through January 9, 2028. While the exhibition establishes the gala’s theme, the evening’s official dress code has not yet been announced.

On July 31, the Met's Costume Institute announced plans for a spring 2027 exhibition devoted to John Galliano, to be launched by that year’s Met Gala. "John Galliano: Horizons," would examine the designer's four-decade career and be the 2027 Met Gala theme. The choice was controversial from the start: Galliano was fired by Dior in 2011 after video surfaced of him making antisemitic and racist remarks in Paris, and was later convicted of a hate crime in France. Curator Andrew Bolton told The New York Times the show would confront that history head-on, calling it "the rupture."

Backlash built through August. City Council Speaker Julie Menin sent the Met a letter calling the decision "a serious mistake,” and Pace Gallery founder Arne Glimcher reportedly threatened to withhold gifts to the museum. Reports also noted the Gala's traditional date, May 3, 2027, would fall on Yom HaShoah (Holocaust Remembrance Day), adding pressure on organizers already said to be weighing a date change. Over the weekend, The New York Times published a story indicating that Galliano had made antisemitic remarks as recently as 2019. Met executives held an emergency meeting on August 30, and on August 31, the Met and Galliano jointly announced the exhibition would not proceed.

In a statement, Max Hollein, the Met's director and CEO, said: "Following thoughtful discussions with John Galliano, we have together decided not to proceed with the exhibition." Galliano said the choice came after "much reflection," adding that he remains "fully accountable for the pain caused by my words in the past, particularly the hurt I caused to the Jewish and Asian communities."

The Met has confirmed that the 2027 Met Gala will take place on Monday, May 3, 2027—the first Monday in May—at the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York.

As of November 5, the gala’s co-chairs, host committee, guest list, and official dress code have not been announced. The Met says more event details will follow, along with information about exhibition-related public programs in 2027.

#6881. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

#6879. When to Arrive at (and Leave) a Party, According to the Pros

What to know about being on the fashionable side of fashionably late.

Once again, we find ourselves on the verge of party season, that most wonderful (and exhausting) time of year. The nights are long and busy; the days are short and tired—and it’s all totally worth it. This season also ushers in the usual quandaries: What to bring, what to wear, and, perhaps the most confusing of all, when to arrive.

Everyone agrees that the best dinner party guests know to give their hosts a bit of time past the official start, but how much is right? And how does that change for a cocktail party? Fear not; we’ve gathered the best and brightest party-goers to answer all those questions: how to be late enough to be fashionable and early enough to be polite, courtesy of those who know best.

ELLE Decor Rising Star Romilly Newman, the chef and food stylist who towers her chocolate cakes as high as her well-decked greenery, thinks “30 minutes is the sweet spot,” she says. “As a frequent host, I'm always running behind and secretly wishing everyone would arrive late. Whether it's running to buy bags of ice, ironing the tablecloth, or an extra braise in the oven.” She warns, however, “An hour late is when it starts to get into dangerous territory—as the host is most likely waiting to serve a hot meal.” But, when it comes to dinner, when it’s time to sit down, it’s also time to stay down: “Cocktail parties are come and go, dinner parties are a nightlong commitment.”

Ivy Getty, a stalwart of guest lists from Milan to Manhattan, suggests arriving a bit earlier: “The best window is 15-20 minutes, so as not to rush the host. I don’t really think of these as rules: they feel like common sense.” She adds: “Obviously, if you are going to a funeral or graduation you arrive early—or on time at the latest.”

Ryan Nordheimer, a New Yorker who plans elaborate monthly party potlucks, suggests that “the sweet spot” for those headed to dinner parties is 5 to 15 minutes after the host tells you to arrive. He adds, “please never be early. Those 5 extra minutes always come in handy as the host.” If it’s a cocktail party, however, particularly one on the longer side, “you can arrive right on time to 30 minutes late.” Plus, he says, “You can always text the host the day before and ask—just don’t ask them the night of.”

Then, when every interesting topic has been exhausted, and any talk about the weather has completely dried up, there’s the question of departures. Newman says, “I like to think of a dinner party like a play in three acts. There's the mingling, the meal, and the lingering. When the host starts clearing the table and blowing out candles, it's time to pack up.”

Nordheimer has a more scientific approach to closing time: on “a weeknight, I like to have people leave around 10pm,” he says. He’ll go an hour later on weekends. “But every host is different. The host reserves the right to kick people out; just start yawning a lot to make it obvious,” he notes. Clear signals—and choosing guests who can pick up on them—is key.

As for how a host should plan, “You should have most of your food done by the time guests arrive,” says Newman. At the end of the day, though, as long as a guest arrives ready to keep the energy high and be gracious to their host, they can’t go wrong.

Dorothy Scarborough (she/her) is the assistant to the Editor in Chief of Town & Country and Elle Decor.

#6874. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

#6870. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

#6869. 5 Fall Trends Designers Think Will Still Look Good Next Spring

Above: A hallway view of Eric Egan’s Milan apartment.

If any season has the power to inspire a complete aesthetic overhaul, it’s fall. Nature’s changing palette inspires us to follow suit, as each year our mood boards make new space for jewel tones, cozy textures, and a bottomless candle budget. Autumnal trends are so alluring, it almost feels like a risky time to make long-term design decisions. But with the right consideration, that seasonal inspiration can translate into choices that look good year-round.

This fall in particular, designers are urging clients to trust their guts—making sure they see themselves in the spaces they create, rather than blindly following the latest fads. “For me, a trend has to be authentic or it’s just noise,” says designer Chad Dorsey, who’s eager to ditch the spare minimalism of years past for looks with more personality. ELLE Decor A-Lister Kerry Joyce seconds the motion: “By prioritizing personalized storytelling, a space becomes evergreen, evolving as smaller objects are swapped out over time.”

But an expert nudge in the right direction never hurts. This fall, top designers are gravitating toward creative color-drenching, cleverly concealed storage, tactile upholstery, and rooms built around art. Read on for their takes on the fall 2026 trends worth investing in, plus tips on building (as Dorsey puts it) “spaces designed to be lived in, not looked at.”

Fabric and upholstery are trending ultra plush and textured for fall, but when thoughtfully integrated, they feel more timeless than seasonal. “Design is shifting toward how a space feels, not just how it looks,” says Joyce, who’s seeing an increased popularity in fabrics like mohair, velvet, cashmere, and alpaca hair. “This focus on texture does more than elevate a room's aesthetic,” he explains. “It creates a sensory experience that comforts the soul.”

Start your bedroom redesign this fall and your space will strike the perfect balance between fresh and lived-in by spring. Between client requests and a personal home build, Amber Lewis has bedrooms on the brain right now. One trend takes the lead in her eyes: “I love matching the walls to the bed upholstery and bedding so the whole room feels wrapped in one warm color family. It’s calm and peaceful, and that enveloping feeling works in any season.”

Luckily, this cozy trend doesn’t require a complete room overhaul: “The easiest place to start is the bed itself. Reupholster your headboard in a pattern you love, or drape a vintage suzani across the foot of the bed,” Lewis says. “One move like that changes the whole mood of the room without starting over.” To bring in a little dimension and pop, she recommends adding a singular statement, like a lone striped pillow.

If your primary suite needs a more holistic update, Lewis recommends creating a small sitting area. Sourcing vintage chairs and small sofas for primary suites this fall has given Lewis a new perspective on where bedroom design is headed: “True sanctuaries that feel layered, personal, and built for actually living in.”

Color drenching isn’t going anywhere for fall 2026—or long after, according to Dorsey. When choosing a shade for a room, he finds that any color works harder when it’s layered and intentional. Color capping—a term coined by Benjamin Moore referring to painting a room in multiple tones from one color family—can help keep a space from feeling flat or overwhelmed beneath a statement hue.

According to Dorsey, the staying power of color drenching goes hand in hand with the shift away from spareness and toward “collected” spaces: “Neither is really a ‘look;’ they’re about living with things you love, which is why they’ll carry well into spring.”

Designer Regan Baker says that cleverly hidden space solutions are also on the rise. “It could be a TV that slides behind a commissioned art panel, a Murphy bed tucked into a wall of millwork or that serves as a desk when it's stored, or a peg wall that’s a place to hang a jacket one minute and a standing desk the next,” she says. These solutions are tailored to her clients’ spaces (rather than tied to a season or palette), which gives them staying power.

This concept is an easy way to turn the corner you’ve never known how to style into your favorite part of the room. “In an existing home, look at the awkward spots, like an alcove or the wall around a fireplace, and treat built-ins there as a design moment instead of an afterthought,” Baker advises.

If there’s one built-to-last fall trend most designers can agree on, it’s added eccentricity. “Homes are steering away from the sterile, overnight look of a showroom delivery,” Joyce says. “The defining philosophy now focuses on layering modern staples with antiques, secondhand finds, travel mementos, and new pieces that carry a sense of patina.”

Dorsey describes this style as “collected rather than prescribed,” where a high-low mix of vintage and custom elements gel together. And while it may feel loftier than a trending, sold-out piece, Baker says that most clients start with an artistic anchor, then build around it: “We’re designing more homes around a piece of art than around a color trend. That might be wallpaper by local designers, a commissioned piece, or even a palette pulled from a lichen-covered rock found on the property.”

One key takeaway? Curate like a collector. Your reward may be a space that never goes out of style.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6868. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: CARBOLocation: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dove on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6866. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

#6861. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6855. Julia Child’s Purple-Hued Cambridge Home Sells Just Days After Listing

The legendary TV chef’s Massachusetts house fetched $6.525 million—above the asking price.

In today’s real estate market, price cuts are increasingly common. But Julia Child’s former Cambridge home just bucked the trend, selling mere days after listing—and fetching above the asking price. The legendary TV chef’s longtime Massachusetts residence hit the market on September 23 for $6.475 million. By October 2, it had found a buyer. The sale officially closed on October 29, per Zillow. In the end, the six-bedroom Cape Cod–style house traded hands for $6.525 million, $50,000 above asking.

Child and her husband, Paul, lived in the house for decades, starting in 1961, after Paul retired from his job in the federal government. The estate is also where Child shot several popular TV shows, among them In Julia’s Kitchen with Master Chefs and Julia and Jacques Cooking at Home.

Painted a muted shade of purple, the 6,300-square-foot home sits on a quarter-acre lot just a few blocks from Harvard Yard. Dating to 1889, the residence at one time featured a custom kitchen designed especially for Child, with pegboard walls, a six-burner industrial Garland range, and maple countertops a few inches taller than average to suit her 6’3” frame.

In the wake of Paul’s 1994 death, Child continued to live in the residence until 2001, when she donated the property to Smith College, her alma mater. (The Massachusetts institution sold the home in 2002 for $2.35 million, per Zillow.) The famous kitchen, meanwhile, was meticulously deconstructed—down to its linoleum flooring—and rebuilt in D.C.’s National Museum of American History, where it remains on display today.

Since then, the new owners have revamped the three-story home’s kitchen, which now sports marble countertops, a metallic backsplash, Poggenpohl cabinetry, and a Sub-Zero refrigerator. The rest of the residence is equipped with light-filled interiors, hardwood floors, and eco-friendly additions like solar panels.

This summer, the Cambridge City Council passed a proposal to install a historical marker on the estate’s grounds, according to Boston.com.

By chance, another Child-linked property recently traded hands as well, albeit for a discount. In August, a Santa Barbara ranch where she filmed the 12-episode PBS series Dinner at Julia’s listed for just under $22 million. It sold on October 16 for $19 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6854. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

#6853. The 25 Sexiest Movies on Prime Video Right Now

Including the best erotic thrillers, raunchy comedies, and all the mess in between.

Even in just the past decade, sexy cinema has come a long way from Fifty Shades of Grey. No judgement here—the entire trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video if you haven’t seen the Dakota Johnson and Jamie Dornan-starring franchise. But if you’re looking to expand your range a bit, we have a lot to recommend.

Sexy movies are having a comeback in 2026, thanks to Wuthering Heights, I Want Your Sex, and—another Olivia Wilde film—The Invite. While Emerald Fennell adapted Emily Bronte’s novel into a tortured and depraved sexual experience, Wilde’s I Want Your Sex and The Invite used sex more as a vehicle for dark comedy. But it’s not just new films on this list. We can also point you to Weekend, Ghost, Before Sunrise, and just any kind of sexy movie you’re looking for on screen.

Below, you’ll find our pick of the 25 sexiest movies you can stream on Prime Video right now.

Olivia Wilde and Cooper Hoffman star in Gregg Araki’s raunchy workplace romantic comedy about a high profile artist (Wilde) who ropes one of her new assistants (Hoffman) into a dominant/submissive relationship. At first, he enjoys the attention, but the rollercoaster of their sexual power dynamic eventually drives them both insane.

Sydney Sweeney does Rear Window meets Peeping Tom in this sexy crime drama about a couple who becomes obsessed with the sex lives of the people they can see from their apartment window across the street. The film also stars Justice Smith and Ahsoka’s Natasha Liu Bordizzo.

No Hard Feelings follows a down-on-her-luck Uber driver (Jennifer Lawrence) who is hired by a very wealthy couple to take their inexperienced nineteen-year-old son (Andrew Barth Feldman) on a date to boost his confidence before he leaves for college. The film treads dangerous waters, but Lawrence won over the audience and earned herself a Golden Globe nomination for the sexy comedy.

Love her or hate her, Emerald Fennell can make a steamy movie. Wuthering Heights is a little more of a reimagining than an adaptation, taking quite a few liberties with the text, but it puts the romantic and sexual dynamics right at the forefront of the story. And there’s no beating Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi as the leads.

The Fault in Our Stars director Josh Boone teams up with It Ends With Us novelist Colleen Hoover for a romance drama about a teen pregnancy that alters the course of two lovers’ lives. That is, until later in life, when the couple (Allison Wiliams and Dave Franco) reunite to explore what could have been.

Emerald Fennell’s steamy The Talented Mr. Ripley–esque thriller, Saltburn, follows a young student at Oxford University who becomes infatuated (read: dangerously obsessed) with one of his aristocratic classmates, who invites him to his family’s grand estate. It’s a darkly funny and wildly intense ride.

The phenom that saved dark romance novels: Fifty Shades of Grey and the entire Fifty Shades trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video. The story, for those who still don’t know, follows a recent college graduate (Dakota Johnson) who signs a contract to take part in a sexual bondage relationship with billionaire Christian Gray (Jamie Dornan).

L’inconnu du lac (known as Stranger by the Lake) is one of the most acclaimed erotic thrillers of the 21st century, picking up Best Director and the Queer Palm awards in the year it premiered at the Cannes Film Festival. In Stranger by the Lake, Franck, a man in his 30s, meets Michel by a lake where gay men often bathe and immediately falls for him. What he doesn’t know, however, is that Michel is a psychopathic murderer. Stranger by the Lake is as smart as it is sexy, and it will truly keep you on your toes.

Who said rom-coms can’t be sexy too? Secretary charts the dominant-submissive relationship that blossoms between a lawyer and his secretary. It’s certainly on the crazier side and may not be for everyone, but Secretary is a brilliantly acted film that feels quite far ahead of its time for such a mainstream movie in its original take on a romantic BDSM relationship.

Carol does something a little different: It’s a slow-burning romance with sensuality bubbling beneath the surface. It follows a department-store worker and aspiring photographer whose life takes an unexpected turn when she falls in love with a mysterious, beautiful older woman going through a difficult divorce. Todd Haynes’s Oscar-nominated period romance is a masterpiece of modern queer cinema, with career-defining performances from Rooney Mara and Cate Blanchett. Hugely romantic and remarkably executed, Carol is a special film that’ll certainly stick with you.

Alongside Four Weddings and a Funeral, Sirens was one of the films that brought generational British heartthrob Hugh Grant to American audiences. Grant plays a progressive reverend who, with his wife, pays a visit to an eccentric artist whose work is viewed as blasphemous. His wife soon finds herself drawn to the three models working with the painter, and what ensues is a clever breakdown of 1930s sexual repression.

Everyone loves a meet-cute, and Weekend’s is as brilliant as they come. Russell thinks he’s signed up for a one-night stand with Glen, a man he meets in a club, but over the course of a single weekend, a deeper relationship quickly grows between the two. Touching, oddly realistic, and distinctly British in its sense of humor, Weekend is sexy, funny, and grounded in a way that few romantic comedy-dramas manage.

In Eternity, Elizabeth Olsen plays a woman who, upon reaching the afterlife, is forced to choose between two people to spend eternity with: her first husband, who died young, and her second husband of 65 years. Eternity is infectiously sweet, a little sexy, and an essential modern comedy.

It might be a stretch to call this one “sexy,” per se, but you won’t find a romantic drama quite like this one—and you definitely won’t find one that’s more intense. Luca Guadagnino’s Bones and All is a road-trip drama that follows a young woman who develops feelings for a disillusioned young drifter on a 3,000-mile journey across America. Oh, and they’re both cannibals.

Wong Kar-wai’s romantic masterpiece proves that a film can be truly romantic, sensual, and intense while still holding solidly on to a PG rating. In the Mood for Love follows a man and a woman who discover that their spouses are having an affair with one another, but as they bond, they begin to develop their own connection. This is one for the yearners.

Sexy films are by no means a new phenomenon, and Caligula is one of the originals. Though the original was pretty poorly received, the film recently received a restoration and rerelease that reconstructed the film entirely from footage that didn’t make the final cut.

Love triangles don’t get much more complicated than this, and sports dramas don’t get much sexier. Challengers follows two friends who fall in love with the same young tennis prodigy and the complicated chaos that ensues when their relationships get messy. Tennis has never looked so thrilling

Set in the dreamy Italian countryside, Call Me by Your Name remains the film that introduced Timothée Chalamet to the masses as a future leading man (and got him his first Oscar nomination). Here he plays a teenager who falls in love with his father’s assistant and comes to terms with his sexuality. The film is sad, symbolic, and deeply romantic.

The legendary leading duo of Patrick Swayze and Demi Moore speaks for itself. Ghost is maybe a little more on the sentimental side than some of the other “sexy films” in Prime Video’s lineup, but it’s a deeply moving one worth your time.

Portrait of a Lady on Fire was an immediate hit with critics and audiences when it was released in 2019. The film follows an artist commissioned to paint an aristocratic heiress, but things become more complicated when the two fall in love. Romantic films don’t get more French than this one, which is also loosely based on the timeless tale of Orpheus and Eurydice.

Nicole Kidman and Harris Dickinson are an absolutely electrifying onscreen pair in this erotic thriller that sees a CEO begin an affair with her power-hungry intern, putting the stability of her family life and career on the line. Kidman picked up the Best Actress award at the Venice Film Festival for her role, and it’s been called one of her most ambitious performances to date.

Can anything capture your heart like a meet-cute romance? American traveler Jesse and French student Céline unceremoniously meet on a train from Budapest to Vienna, deciding on a whim to spend his last hours on the continent together. It’s the first in Richard Linklater’s acclaimed Before trilogy, and it sets you up for a brilliant slow burn.

If a Skarsgård signed up for it, I’m interested. He’s about to play a love interest made out of wicker, but last year Alexander Skarsgård played the mysteriously sexy biker-gang leader Ray in Pillion. The romantic black comedy tells the story of a shy man who begins a dominant-submissive relationship with the aforementioned biker, and it traces his personal growth as he navigates their dynamic. Pillion is sexy, crazy, and downright hilarious. It’s British black comedy at its finest.

Starring the king of crazy, surprisingly sexy indie movies, James Spader himself, Sex, Lies and Videotape is a true cult classic. The film revolves around a married couple with intimacy problems whose lives are changed when a mysterious drifter with a peculiar interest comes to stay with them.

David Cronenberg’s notorious erotic thriller is perhaps the most transgressive entry on this list, following a group of people who become sexually fixated on car crashes. Adapted from J.G. Ballard’s novel and starring James Spader, Holly Hunter, and Deborah Kara Unger, Crash is disturbing, provocative, and about as daring as erotic cinema gets.

#6848. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.
